成功值得吗？

Is Success Worth It?

克里斯：幸福是对你已有的东西感到满足。成功来自不满足。那成功还值得吗？

Chris: Happiness is being satisfied with what you have. Success comes from dissatisfaction. Is success worth it then?

纳瓦尔：唉。我不确定那句话现在还成立。那是我很久以前写下的。这类话很多都只是写给自己的便条，而且非常依赖情境。它们在当下出现，也在当下离开。

Naval: Oof. I'm not sure that statement is true anymore. Like, I made that statement a long time ago, and a lot of these things are just notes to myself, and they're highly contextual. They come in the moment, they leave in the moment.

幸福。好。这是个很复杂的话题。不过我一直喜欢苏格拉底那个故事：他走进集市，人家把各种奢侈品、精致玩意儿指给他看，他说：「这世上有多少东西是我不想要的。」对吧？那也是一种自由。所以，不想要一样东西，和拥有它一样好。

Happiness. Okay. So, very complicated topic. But I always like the Socrates story, where he goes into the marketplace and they show him all these luxuries and fineries, and he says, “How many things there are in this world that I do not want.” Right? And that's a form of freedom. So not wanting something is as good as having it.

还有亚历山大大帝和狄奥尼西奥斯那个老故事——对，亚历山大出去征服了世界，遇见住在木桶里的狄奥尼西奥斯。狄奥尼西奥斯说：「走开，你挡住我的太阳了。」亚历山大说：「啊，来生我多么希望，你知道，能像狄奥尼西奥斯一样。」狄奥尼西奥斯说：「差别就在这里。我并不希望能——」抱歉，是第欧根尼，第欧根尼。第欧根尼说：「我并不希望成为亚历山大。」

In the old story with Alexander and Dionysius—right, Alexander goes out and conquers the world, and he meets Dionysius, who's living in a barrel, and Dionysius says, “Get out of the way, you're blocking my sun.” And Alexander says, “Oh, how I wish I, you know, could be like Dionysius in the next life.” And Dionysius says, “That's the difference. I don't wish that I could—” sorry, Diogenes, Diogenes. Diogenes says, “I don't wish to be Alexander.”

所以通往幸福有两条路。一条是成功：你得到想要的，满足物质需要。或者像第欧根尼那样，从一开始就不要。我不确定哪一条更站得住。这也取决于你怎么定义成功。如果最终目标是幸福，那为什么不直奔主题、直接去追求它？幸福会让你更不成功吗？

So two paths to happiness. One path is success: you get what you want, you satisfy your material needs. Or, like Diogenes, you just don't want it in the first place. And I'm not sure which one is more valid. And it also depends what you define as success. If the end goal is happiness, then why not cut to the chase and just go straight for it? Does being happy make you less successful?

那是一种通行看法。那甚至可能是你从现实里挣来的切实经验。你发现自己一幸福，就什么也不想要了，于是你不起来做任何事。另一方面，你知道，你还是得做点什么。你是动物。你在这里。你在这里是为了生存，为了繁衍。你被驱动，你有动机，你会去做点什么。你不会整天就那么坐着。不太可能。有些人会。也许那是他们的天性。但我觉得大多数人还是想行动。他们想活在竞技场里。

That is a conventional wisdom. That may even be the practical earned experience of your reality. You find that when you're happy, you don't want anything. So you don't get up and do anything. On the other hand, you know, you still got to do something. You're an animal. You're here. You're here to survive. You're here to replicate. You're driven. You're motivated. You're going to do something. You're not just going to sit there all day. Unlikely. Some people do. Maybe it's in their nature. But I think most people still want to act. They want to live in the arena.

我发现就我自己而言，当我变得——「更幸福」这个词太大了，不过你知道，更平静、更安定、更在场、对已有的更满足——我仍然想做事。我只是想做更大的事。我想做更纯粹的事，更贴近我认为需要做成、并且只有我能做成的事。所以从这个意义上说，我觉得更幸福其实能让你更成功，但你对成功的定义多半会一路改变。

I found for myself, as I've become—happier is a big word, but you know, more peaceful, more calm, more present, more satisfied with what I have—I still want to do things. I just want to do bigger things. I want to do things that are more pure, more aligned with what I think needs to be done and what I can uniquely do. So in that sense, I think that being happier can actually make you more successful, but your definition of success will likely change along the way.

克里斯：你觉得，如果当初没有先获得一些成功，你能到达这种认识吗？

Chris: Is that a realization you think you could have gotten to had you not had some success in the first place?

纳瓦尔：至少对我来说，我一直想先走物质成功这条路。我不会去当苦行者，坐在那儿弃绝一切。那看起来太不现实，也太痛苦。在佛陀的故事里，他先是王子，然后看到这一切都有点无意义，因为你还是会老、会死去，于是他走进树林去寻找更多的东西。我选择那条包含物质成功的幸福路线。谢谢。我觉得某种程度上这条更快。

Naval: At least for me, I always wanted to take the path of material success first. I was not going to go be an ascetic and sit there and renounce everything. That just seems too unrealistic and too painful. In the story of Buddha, he starts out as a prince, and then he sees that it's all kind of meaningless because you're still going to get old and die, and then he goes into the woods looking for something more. I'll take the happy route that involves material success. Thank you. I think it's quicker in some ways.

克里斯：你有一个洞见是：实现我们的物质欲望，远比弃绝它们容易。

Chris: You know, one of your insights is: it's far easier to achieve our material desires than it is to renounce them.

纳瓦尔：这因人而异，但我觉得你必须去试那条路。你想要什么，就去把它拿来。我曾经打趣说，赢下这场游戏，是为了从它里面解脱出来。所以你玩游戏，赢下游戏，然后——但愿——你会对这些游戏感到厌烦。你不会只想在同一场游戏里一圈圈循环。尽管其中很多游戏非常诱人，有很多关卡，也相对开放。然后你从游戏里解脱出来，意思是你不再试图赢它。你知道，你能赢。然后你要么换一场游戏，要么纯粹为了乐趣去玩。

Naval: And it depends on the person, but I think you have to try that path. If you want something, go get it. You know, I quipped that the reason to win the game is to be free of it. So you play the games, you win the games, and then you get—hopefully you get bored of the games. You don't want to just keep looping on the same game over and over. Although a lot of these games are very enticing and have many levels and are relatively open-ended. And then you become free of the game, in a sense that you're no longer trying to win it. You know, you can win it. And either you move to a different game, or you play the game for the sheer joy of it.

克里斯：你还有另一句：生命里大部分收获，来自短期受苦，好在长期得到回报。我觉得这是经典说法。每天都通过棉花糖测验。但有个有趣的难题：我觉得人们需要避免变成受苦成瘾者。把受苦本身当作进步的代理指标，而不是看受苦带来的结果。就像：我不吃那颗棉花糖时很痛苦。我做这份功课时很痛苦。我把安好和满足挂在了痛苦上，而不是挂在痛苦另一边给我的东西上。

Chris: You—another one of yours: most of the gains in life come from suffering in the short term so you can get paid in the long term. I think that's classic. Winning the marshmallow test on a daily basis. But there's an interesting challenge, where I think people need to avoid becoming a suffering addict. Sort of using suffering as the proxy for progress, as opposed to the outcome of the suffering. It's like: I was in pain not eating the marshmallow. I was in pain doing this work. I have attached well-being and satisfaction to pain, not to what the pain gets me on the other side of it.

纳瓦尔：如果你把痛定义成肉体上的痛，那它是真东西。它会发生，你无法无视。但我们说的苦不是那个。苦多半是精神上的煎熬和精神上的痛。它只是意味着你不想做手头这件事。如果你做手头这件事觉得没问题，你就不会苦。接下来的问题是：哪一种更有效——一路受苦，还是干脆用一种不把它当成苦的方式来理解它？你听到很多成功的人回头说：哦，旅程才是有趣的部分，对吧？那其实才是好玩的部分，我当时该多享受一些。这是一种常见的遗憾。

Naval: If you define pain as physical pain, then it's a real thing. It happens and you can't ignore it. But that's not what we mean by suffering. Suffering is mostly mental anguish and mental pain. And it just means you don't want to do the task at hand. If you were fine doing the task at hand, then you wouldn't be suffering. And then the question is: what's more effective—to suffer along the way, or just to interpret it in a way that it's not suffering? You hear from a lot of successful people, they look back and they say, oh, the journey was the fun part, right? That was actually the entertaining part, and I should have enjoyed it more. It's a common regret.

我喜欢做一个小小的思想练习：你可以回到自己的人生里，试着把自己放进整整五年前、十年前、十五年前、二十年前的那个位置。你试着记得：好，当时我和谁在一起？我在做什么？我有什么感觉？我的情绪是什么？我的目标是什么？然后真的、真的试着把自己送回去，看看有没有任何建议要给当时的自己——有没有什么事你会做得不一样。注意，你并没有新的信息。也不要假装你可以回去买一只股票、买比特币或别的什么。只是凭你现在对自己性情的了解，再加上一点点和年龄有关的经验，你会怎样把事情做得不一样？我觉得这是个值得做的练习。

There's a little thought exercise I like to do, which is: you can go back into your own life and try to put yourself in the exact position you were in 5 years ago, 10 years ago, 15 years ago, 20 years ago. And you try to remember: okay, who was I with? What was I doing? What was I feeling? What were my emotions? What were my objectives? And really, really try to transport yourself back, and see if there's any advice you'd give yourself—anything you would do differently. Now, you don't have new information. And don't pretend you could have gone back and, you know, bought a stock or bought Bitcoin or whatever. But just knowing what you know now, in terms of your temperament and a little bit of age-related experience, how would you have done things differently? And I think it's a worthwhile exercise to do.

所以不要让我把结论从你那儿抢走，不过我告诉你，对我来说：所有事我还是会照做，只是会少一点愤怒、少一点情绪、少一点内在的苦，因为那些是可选的。并不是必要的。而且我愿意说，一个至少能平静地、也许还能高兴地做这件事的人，会比一个带着不必要情绪动荡的人更有效。

So don't let me rob you of the conclusion, but I'll tell you for me: I would have done everything the same, except I would have done it with less anger, less emotion, less internal suffering, because that was optional. It wasn't necessary. And I would argue that someone who can do the job at least peacefully, but maybe happily, is going to be more effective than someone who has unnecessary emotional turmoil.

克里斯：那你最后就会得到一连串悲惨的成功，对吧？结果也许一样，但到达那里的整个体验——旅程不只是报酬。旅程是仅有的东西。

Chris: Well, you end up with a series of miserable successes, right? The outcome may have been the same, but the entire experience of getting there—and the journey is not only the reward. The journey is the only thing there is.

纳瓦尔：你知道，即便是成功——人的天性是非常、非常快地把它存进账户、习以为常，对吧？因为我们跑的那个常规循环是：你闲坐着，觉得无聊，然后你想要一样东西；一旦想要，你就决定在得到它之前都不会幸福。然后你开始一轮受苦或期待，同时努力去把那东西拿来。如果你得到了，你就会习惯它，然后又觉得无聊。几个月后，你又想要别的东西。如果你没得到，你会不高兴一阵子，然后走出来，然后又想要别的。对吧？那就是常规周期。所以无论你最后幸福还是不幸福，往往都不会持久。

Naval: You know, even success—it's human nature to bank it very, very quickly, right? Because the normal loop that we run through is: you sit around, you're bored, then you want something; then when you want something, you decide you're not going to be happy until you get that thing. Then you start your bout of suffering or anticipation while you strive to get that thing. If you get that thing, then you get used to it, and then you get bored again. Then a few months later, you want something else. And if you don't get it, then you're unhappy for a bit, and then you get over it, and then you want something else. Right? That's the normal cycle. So whether you're happy or unhappy at the end, it tends not to last.

我并不想油嘴滑舌地说：哦，赚钱或成功没有意义。绝对有意义。钱能解决你所有的钱的问题。所以有钱是好的。话虽如此，有那些故事——我不知道你有没有看过那些研究。我不知道它们有多真实。很多心理学研究无法复现，不过有个好玩的小研究说：摔断脊背的人和中彩票的人，两年后都会回到原来的幸福基线。

Now I don't want to be glib and say that, oh, there's no point in making money or being successful. There absolutely is. Money solves all your money problems. So it is good to have money. That said, there are those stories—I don't know if you've seen those studies. I don't know how real these are. A lot of these psych studies don't replicate, but it's a fun little study that shows that people who break their back and people who win the lottery are back to their baseline happiness two years later.

克里斯：对。

Chris: Yep.

纳瓦尔：再说一遍，我不知道那是不是完全属实。我觉得如果你是自己挣来的，钱能买到幸福，因为一路上你既有对自己的骄傲和信心，也有一种成就感，你着手去做一件事，而且你是对的。所以我打赌那种东西会留下来。然后就像我说的，钱能解决你的钱的问题。所以我不想在这一点上太轻飘。但一般说来，我们跑的这个欲望、多巴胺、满足、不满足的循环——你必须享受旅程。旅程就是全部，对吧？你99%的时间都花在旅途上。所以，如果你不打算享受它，那还算怎样的一段旅程？

Naval: Again, I don't know if that's entirely true. I think money can buy you happiness if you earned it, because then along the way you have both pride and confidence in yourself, and you have a sense of accomplishment, and you set out to do something and you were right. So I'll bet that lingers. And then as I said, money solves your money problem. So I don't want to be too glib about it. But I would say in general, this loop that we run through of desire, dopamine, fulfillment, unfulfillment—like, you have to enjoy the journey. The journey is all there is, right? 99% of your time is spent on the journey. So what kind of a journey is it if you're not going to enjoy it?

缩短欲望的方法

Ways To Shortcut Our Desires

克里斯：你怎么走那条欲望契约的捷径？

Chris: How do you shortcut that desire contract?

纳瓦尔：你可以聚焦。你可以决定：大多数东西我都不要。我觉得我们有很多不必要的欲望，到处随手捡来。我们对一切都有看法，对一切都下判断。所以我觉得，只要知道那些是不幸福的源头，你就会对欲望变得挑剔。而且坦白说，如果你想成功，你必须对欲望挑剔。你必须聚焦。你不可能样样出色。那样你只会浪费精力、浪费时间。

Naval: You could focus. You could decide that I don't want most things. I think we have a lot of unnecessary desires that we just pick up everywhere. We have opinions on everything, judgments on everything. So I think just knowing that those are the source of unhappiness will make you be choosy about your desires. And frankly, if you want to be successful, you have to be choosy about your desires. You have to focus. You can't be great at everything. You're just going to waste your energy and waste your time.

克里斯：名声是一个值得追求的目标吗？

Chris: Is fame a worthwhile goal?

纳瓦尔：它让你被邀请去更好的派对，让你进更好的餐厅。名声——名声是件奇怪的事：很多人认识你，你却不认识他们。它确实会把你放到台座上。它可以让你隔着一段距离得到想要的东西。所以我不会说它毫无价值。显然，人们想要它是有原因的。它是高地位，所以吸引异性。尤其对男人来说，它吸引女人。话虽如此，代价很高。意味着你没有隐私。你确实会碰上怪人、疯子。你确实会常常被奇怪的事情缠上。而且你在舞台上，所以你被迫表演，被迫和自己过去的宣言与行动保持一致，你还会有黑粉以及诸如此类的废话。

Naval: It gets you invited to better parties, gets you into better restaurants. Fame—so fame is this funny thing where a lot of people know you, but you don't know them. And it does get you put on a pedestal. It can get you what you want, at a distance. So I wouldn't say it's worthless. Obviously, people want it for a reason. It's high status, so it attracts the opposite sex. Especially for men, it attracts women. That said, it is high cost. It means you have no privacy. You do have weirdos and lunatics. You do get hit up a lot for weird things. And you're on a stage, so you're forced to perform, so you're forced to be consistent with your past proclamations and actions, and you're going to have haters and all that nonsense.

但我们确实在这么做，我们似乎都想要它——这意味着，如果说「哦，不，不，我很有名——可你并不想出名」，就会显得不真诚。话虽如此，我觉得名声和其他东西一样，最好作为某种可能更值得的事情的副产品被产生出来，或被追求。想出名、渴求出名、因为出名而出名：这些多少都是陷阱。

But the fact that we do it, the fact that we all seem to want it, means that it would be disingenuous to say, “Oh, no, no, I'm famous—but you don't want to be famous.” That said, I think fame, like anything else, is best produced as a—or pursued as a—byproduct of something potentially more worthwhile. Wanting to be famous and craving to be famous and being famous for being famous: these are sort of traps.

克里斯：为名声而名声。

Chris: Fame for fame's sake.

纳瓦尔：对。正是。所以更好的是挣来的名声。比如说，在部落里赢得尊敬，是你做对部落有好处的事。人类历史上最有名的人是谁？是那些某种程度上超越了自我的人。世上的佛陀、耶稣、穆罕默德。还有谁有名？艺术家有名。艺术能流传很久。科学家有名。他们发现事物。征服者有名，大概是因为他们为自己的部落去征服。他们是在为某个人而战。

Naval: Yeah. Exactly. So it's better that it's earned fame. So for example, to earn respect in the tribe is you do things that are good for the tribe. Who are the most famous people in human history? There are people who sort of transcended the self. The Buddhas and the Jesuses and the Muhammads of the world. Who else is famous? The artists are famous. You know, art lasts for a long time. The scientists are famous. They discover things. The conquerors are famous, presumably because they conquered for their tribe. There was someone that they were fighting for.

所以一般来说，你通过为越来越大的人群做事而升得越高——即便那可能被视为暴虐或负面，比如成吉思汗很有名，但对蒙古人来说他是在做好事，对其余的人则不尽然——你运作的层面越高，你照料的人越多，你就越是挣来尊敬和名声。我觉得那些是做出名的好理由。如果名声是空的，如果你出名只是因为名字出现在很多地方，或脸出现在很多地方，那就是空洞的名声，而且我觉得你内心深处会知道这一点，于是它会很脆弱，你会永远害怕失去它，然后你就会被迫表演。所以那种纯粹演员和名流拥有的名声，我不会想要。但那种因为你做了有用的事而挣来的名声——为什么要躲开它？

So generally, the higher up you rise by doing things for greater and greater groups of people—even though it may be considered tyrannical or negative, like, you know, Genghis Khan is famous, but to the Mongols he was doing good, to the rest of them not so much—the higher level you're operating at, the more people you're taking care of, the more you sort of earn respect and fame. And I think those are good reasons to be famous. If fame is empty, if you're famous just 'cause your name showed up in a lot of places or your face showed up in a lot of places, then that's a hollow fame, and I think deep down you will know that, and so it'll be fragile and you'll always be afraid of losing it, and then you'll be forced to perform. So the kind of fame that pure actors and celebrities have, I wouldn't want. But the kind of fame that's earned because you did something useful—why dodge that?

改变看法算不算虚伪？

Is Changing Our Opinions Hypocritical?

克里斯：不过——我觉得有个难题，尤其是当人们就某些事发出非常响亮的公开宣言时。你刚才提到，你几乎成了自己从前说过的话的人质：能够更新看法、改变主意，在互联网看来，和虚伪非常像。

Chris: Now—there's a challenge, I think, especially if people make very loud public proclamations about things. You mentioned there that you're almost hostage to the things that you used to say: that being able to update your opinions and change your mind looks, to the internet, very similar to how hypocrisy does.

纳瓦尔：不，不，不。我过去说一样东西、现在说不一样的东西，其间的差别也许是我学到了，也许是我更新了自己的信念。但很少有人用正当的方式这么做。

Naval: No, no, no. The difference between me saying something in the past and saying something different now is perhaps I've learned, perhaps I've updated my beliefs. But so few people do it in a legitimate way.

克里斯：我觉得骗子、托儿——你看，这就是那支冒烟的枪，表明他从头到尾并没有真正相信那件事。

Chris: I think that the grifter, the shill—you see this is the smoking gun that shows that he didn't really believe that thing all along.

纳瓦尔：对。

Naval: Right.

克里斯：对，几年前我去洛杉矶参加过一次静修，有个我以前关注的人，一个做商业和效率建议的内容创作者，非常非常成功，他彻底从一切里退了出来，进入一种像僧侣模式的状态，专注于自己的生意。我问他为什么，他说：我开始觉得，我必须在私下也活成我在公开场合说的那个人。

Chris: And yeah, I went to a retreat in LA a couple of years ago, and there was a guy that I used to follow, a big business and productivity advice content creator, really, really successful, and he just totally stepped back from everything and went like monk mode and focused on his business. I asked him why, and he said, I started feeling like I had to live up to in private the things that I was saying in public.

纳瓦尔：对。是的。这是——那句话是谁说的？是爱默生：愚蠢的一致性，是小心眼的鬼魅，对吧？但本质上，你看，所有生命——所有学习都是纠错，对吧？每一个创造知识的系统，都是通过纠正错误来运作的：做出猜测，再纠正错误。所以从定义上说，如果你在学习，你大多数时候都会错，并且你会更新自己的先验。

Naval: Right. Yeah. It's a—what was it that—who said it? It was Emerson: that a foolish consistency is the hobgoblin of little minds, right? But essentially, look, all life is—all learning is error correction, right? Every knowledge-creation system works through correcting errors: making guesses and correcting errors. So by definition, if you're learning, you're going to be wrong most of the time, and you'll be updating your priors.

比如说，我上过乔·罗根那个播客——我不知道，大概八九年前——人们会揪出那一件后来没有被证明正确的事，对吧？然后他们就一个劲砸这个点，因为这能在他们心里把自己的地位稍微抬高一点。啊哈，我抓到他一个错。我觉得，如果你抓到某人赤裸裸撒谎，他信的是一回事、说的是另一回事，那是正当的。那是性格缺陷。他们不该撒谎。但另一方面，如果他们只是对某件事做了一个猜测，并且猜错了——顺便说，多半是关于人工智能、通用人工智能那件事，而我觉得我在那一点上仍然是对的，不过那是另一个故事。那些以为我们已经实现了通用人工智能的人，只是从他们那一侧没有通过图灵测试。

So for example, I did this Joe Rogan podcast—I don't know, it's like eight or nine years ago—and people will call out like the one thing that didn't turn out to be correct, right? And it's just like—and they just beat on it, because it helps them in their mind raise their status a little bit. Aha, I caught him in an error. Well, I think if you catch someone in a blatant lie, where they believe one thing and they say another, that's legit. That's a character flaw. They shouldn't be lying. But on the other hand, if they just made a guess at something and they got it wrong—and by the way, mostly it's about the AI, AGI thing, and I think I'm still right about that, but it's a different story. People who think we have achieved AGI just fail a Turing test from their side.

不过很有意思，人们会死死抓住单次宣言。可现实是，我们全都是动态系统。我们一直在变，一直在学，一直在长。但愿我们也一直在纠错。你不想做的是：因为想让自己好看而在公开场合撒谎。我觉得人们闻得出那个。眼下这个世界真正缺的是真诚，因为每个人都想要点什么。他们想被看成某种人。他们想成为自己并不是的那种人。于是你确实会抓到很多人说他们并不真正相信的话。我觉得人们对这个非常敏感。识破胡说的雷达已经变得过敏，好判断这个人是不是真的意思就是他说的那样。

But it's funny how people latch on to single proclamations. But the reality is all of us are dynamical systems. We're always changing. We're always learning. We're always growing. And hopefully we're correcting errors. What you don't want to be doing is lying in public because you're trying to look good. And I think people can smell that. What this world really lacks right now is authenticity, because everybody wants something. They want to be seen as something. They want to be something that they're not. And so you do catch a lot of people saying things that they don't really believe. And I think people are very sensitive to that. Bullshit radars have become hypersensitized to try and work out whether or not this person means the thing that they're saying.

克里斯：对。我是说，很多人是错的。我们大多数人在大多数时候都是错的，尤其是在任何新的事业里。不过，错和虚伪是有差别的——是故意错。

Chris: Yeah. I mean, a lot of people are wrong. Most of us are wrong most of the time, especially in any new endeavor. Difference between being wrong and disingenuous, though—purposefully wrong.

纳瓦尔：对。正是。所以我觉得那才是大差别。如果有人错了，没什么大不了，只要他们说出那句话、或持有那个信念，有真诚的理由。但如果他们撒谎是为了抬高地位或外表，或为了符合某种期待，那才是错误。而且那不只是对听者的错误，也是对他们自己的错误，因为那样你就会被困在镜厅里。你自己将会和过去的宣言保持一致。所以如果你对别人撒谎，你也会对自己撒谎。

Naval: Correct. Exactly. So I think that's the big difference. If someone is wrong, no big deal, as long as they have a genuine reason for saying what they're saying, or believing what they're believing. But if they are lying to elevate their status or their appearance, or to live up to some expectation, that's the mistake. And that's a mistake not just for the listener, it's a mistake for themselves, 'cause then you're going to get trapped in a hall of mirrors. You yourself are going to be consistent with your past proclamations. So if you're lying to others, you're going to be lying to yourself.

克里斯：你会被一个甚至都不是你的人牵线操纵。

Chris: You're puppeted by a person that you are not even.

纳瓦尔：没错。是的。就像——那句话怎么说的？你基本上是在试图打动那些你知道并不在乎你的人。所以他们不喜欢真实的你；如果他们看见真实的你，他们也不会在乎；而那些会喜欢真实的你的人，又看不到真实的你，于是他们与你擦肩而过，对吧？你只该要那极少数你所尊敬的人的尊敬。向大众索取尊敬，是傻瓜的差事。

Naval: That's right. Yeah. It's like—what was that line? You're basically trying to impress people who you know don't care about you. So they don't like the real you, and if they saw the real you they wouldn't care, and the people who would like the real you don't get to see the real you, so they pass you by, right? You only want the respect of the very, very few people that you respect. Trying to demand respect from the masses is a fool's errand.

如何少被地位游戏分心

How To Become Less Distracted By Status Games

克里斯：地位游戏——那种积攒的诱惑，无论是名声、真正的出名，还是仅仅那种竞争比较的陷阱——它一直都在。被社会认可牵着走，有一种真实的吸引力。人们该怎样学会少被这样的地位游戏分心？

Chris: Status games—the allure of accruing, whether it's fame, actual fame, or just the competition-comparison trap—it's always there. There's a real draw of being swayed by social approval. How should people learn to get less distracted by status games in that way?

纳瓦尔：我觉得，只要看出地位游戏并不像从前那么要紧，就会有帮助。在旧社会——让我们回到狩猎采集时代——没有财富这种东西。你只有能随身带走的。没有储存起来的财富。所以财富游戏其实不存在，创造财富的游戏不存在。存在的只有地位游戏。如果你地位高，那仅有的一点东西就先到你手里。但即便在那时，你也必须通过照料部落来挣得地位。

Naval: I think it just helps to see that status games don't matter as much as they used to. In old society—let's go back to hunter-gatherer times—there was no such thing as wealth. You just had what you could carry. There was no stored wealth. So wealth games didn't really exist, wealth-creation games. All that existed was status games. If you were high status, then you got what little was available first. But even back then, you had to earn your status by taking care of the tribe.

现在我们有了财富创造：你真的可以创造一种产品或服务，可以把它规模化，可以为很多人提供丰裕。那不是零和，那是正和游戏。我可以富有，你可以富有，我们可以一起创造东西。而且很清楚，既然我们作为一个整体远、远比狩猎采集时代更富有，财富创造是正向的。但地位是有限的。能分的地位有限。它是一个排名梯子。它是一种等级。所以一次地位上升，就有别人地位下降。你当然可以有多种地位，所以某些种类的地位可以扩展，但它不像财富创造可以无限延伸，不像我们可以都住在星辰里、月球基地或火星殖民地之类。

Now we have wealth creation, where you can actually create a product or a service, you can scale that product or service, and you can provide abundance for a lot of people. And that's not zero-sum, that's a positive-sum game. I can be wealthy, you can be wealthy, we can create things together. And clearly, since we are all collectively far, far wealthier than we were in hunter-gatherer times, wealth creation is positive. But status is limited. There's limited status to go around. It's a ranking ladder. It's a hierarchy. And so if it's a rise in status, somebody else has a lowering in status. Now you can have multiple kinds of status, so you can expand some kinds of status, but it's not like wealth creation, where it can go infinitely, where we can all be, you know, living in the stars and moon bases or Mars colonies or what have you.

所以只要意识到：地位游戏天生就是有限的。它们总是对抗性的。它们总是需要直接交锋；而创造财富的游戏可以只是你在创造产品。你不必和任何别人打架。是的，在市场上你的产品必须成功，但那和辱骂别人、生别人的气、觉得被往下压或往上抬、或和某人结怨，并不完全一样。所以我愿意说，创造财富的游戏既更愉快——它们是正和的——并且它们确实有具体的物质回报。你钱更多，就能买更多。

So just realize that status games are inherently limited. They're always combative. They always require direct combat, whereas wealth-creation games can be just you're creating products. You don't have to fight anybody else. Yes, in the marketplace your product has to succeed, but that's not quite the same as invective against other people, or being angry with other people, or feeling pushed down or pushed up, or having a beef with somebody. So I would argue that wealth-creation games are both more pleasant—they're positive-sum—and they actually have concrete material returns. If you have more money, you can buy more.

克里斯：你给我看看，地位能在哪家银行兑换。

Chris: Show me where you can exchange your status at the bank.

纳瓦尔：正是。对。它模糊，也不确定。现在你看人们致富了，他们有钱——他们想要什么？他们想要地位。于是他们去好莱坞，开始演电影，他们向非营利组织捐款，他们去戛纳或达沃斯之类的地方。他们开始试图用钱换地位。所以，你知道，人们总想要自己没有的东西。而我们在进化上被硬连线成追求地位，因为就像我说的，财富创造直到农业革命才能真正存在——那时你可以储存谷物——然后工业革命把它带到另一个层次，现在信息时代又把它带到再一个层次。

Naval: Exactly. Yeah. It's vague and it's fuzzy. Now, you see people get rich, they have money—what do they want? They want status. So they go to Hollywood, start starring in movies, they donate to nonprofits, they go to Cannes or Davos or what have you. And they start trying to trade the money for status. So, you know, people always want what they don't have. And we are evolutionarily hardwired for status, because as I said, wealth creation didn't really exist until the agricultural revolution, when you could store grain, and then the industrial revolution took it to another level, and now the information age is taking it to yet another level.

但从来没有比现在更容易赚钱的时候。是的，它仍然很难，但从来没有比现在更容易创造财富的时候，因为外面有这么多杠杆。有这么多机会。你仍然得自己去找。它不容易。它不会掉到你膝盖上，你必须学点东西、懂点东西、做点有意思的事。但尽管如此，它对多得多的人成为可能。几百年前，你生下来是农奴，就会作为农奴死去。几乎没有出路。这一点已经变了。所以我愿意说，你最好把注意力放在财富游戏上，而不是地位游戏上。如果你试图——比如说——在社交网络上攒粉丝、出名，再靠出名致富，那条路比先致富、然后再去求名声要难得多。这会是我的建议。

But there's never been an easier time to make money. Yes, it's still hard, but there's never been an easier time to create wealth, because there's so much leverage out there. There's so much opportunity. You still have to go find it. It's not easy. It's not going to fall on your lap, and you have to learn something and know something and do something interesting. But nevertheless, it's possible to many more people. A few hundred years ago, you were born a serf, you were going to die a serf. There was almost no way out of that. That's changed. And so I would argue that you're better off focusing on wealth games than status games. If you're trying to, for example, build up your following on a social network and get famous and then get rich off of being famous, that's a much harder path than getting rich first, and then go for your fame afterwards, would be my advice.

克里斯：不过很多人就是这么做的，就像你说的。有意思的是，有些人已经达到那样一种财富水平，你会想——你为什么还需要地位？毕竟大多数人用地位，是为了再把它兑现成财富。如果你已经挣到了可以说「去你的」的钱，如果你已经过了为钱发愁的阶段，或者像人们说的那样「资产很重」——你为什么还要朝相反方向走？

Chris: Well, a lot of people do that, as you said. It's funny how people who have achieved such a level of wealth that you don't think—why do you need the status, given that most people use status to then try and cash in to achieve wealth? If you've achieved fuck-you money already, if you're post-money, or asset-heavy as it's known—why are you trying to go in the other direction?

纳瓦尔：嗯，就像你说的，因为我们在生物上有一段光辉的「想要地位」的历史，而财富相对新鲜。它是新的。

Naval: Well, as you said, because we've got an illustrious history biologically of wanting status, and wealth is kind of novel. It's new.

克里斯：它是新的。

Chris: It's new.

纳瓦尔：财富是一种你必须更多用智性去理解的东西。是的，它有物理的一面：更多食物，更多生存。但要真正理解财富的效果、力量、能力和限度，以及它的优势和劣势，你必须更多地动用新皮质。

Naval: Wealth is something that you have to understand more intellectually. Yeah, there's a physical component: more food, more survival. But to truly understand the effects and the powers and the abilities and limitations, and the advantages and disadvantages of wealth, you have to use your neocortex a lot more.

克里斯：那是不是意味着它不是边缘系统的？玩游戏是为了赢下游戏然后结束它——就「赢了就结束」而言，地位比财富更难。

Chris: Does that mean it's not limbic? The reason to play the game is to win the game and be done with it, is harder to win-and-be-done-with for status than it is for wealth.

纳瓦尔：这是个好观察。我之前没有把这一点想透，但你是对的。对，我觉得那是对的。我觉得人们会永远想要更多地位。但我觉得你可以在某一财富水平上感到满足。

Naval: That's a good observation. I hadn't thought that through, but you're right. Yeah, I think that's right. I think people will always want more status. But I think you can be satisfied at a certain level of wealth.

克里斯：而且，你也总有这样一种感觉。排行榜就是这个，对吧？这就是公告牌排行榜，对吧？它是零和的，也是——我想——福布斯全球富豪榜那种东西。那种梯子更难往上爬。但事实是，比如说，iTunes 和 YouTube 可以让你每天都和同时代的人竞争，让你上上下下，给你看点赞、评论和评分——这就是你升了多少。

Chris: Well, as well, you always have this sort of sense. This is what leaderboards are, right? This is the Billboard chart, right? And it is zero-sum, and it is, I guess, you know, the Forbes richest people on the planet thing. That one's harder to climb the ladder on. But the fact that, for example, iTunes and YouTube can put you in competition against your contemporaries every single day, and make you go up and down, and show you likes and comments and ratings—this is how much you're up.

纳瓦尔：正是。它们让你永远在那台跑步机上跑。

Naval: Exactly. They keep you running on that treadmill forever.

克里斯：吉米·卡尔有个很妙的想法，他说轨迹比位置更重要。所以如果你是世界第101，但去年是第200，对比你是世界第2，但去年是第1——那种减速的感觉非常、非常具体。而这又回到进化。你知道，在流血的东西，除非你止住血，否则最终会死去。所以你被硬连线成不失去已有的东西。而且因为我们进化于那样一种条件：我们离活不下去那么近，你不愿意放弃任何东西。不放弃任何东西，已经被硬连进我们体内。所以你抓得很紧。

Chris: Jimmy Carr has this cool idea where he says trajectory is more important than position. So if you are number 101 in the world, but last year you were number 200, versus you're number two in the world, but last year you were number one—there is this sense of the deceleration is very, very tangible. And it's again—it goes back to evolution. You know, something that is bleeding eventually dies unless you stop the bleeding. So you're hardwired not to lose what you have. And because we evolved in conditions where we're so close to just not surviving, you don't want to give anything up. It's hardwired into us to not give anything up. So you grip tightly.

纳瓦尔：没错。

Naval: That's right.

提升自尊的方法

Ways To Raise Your Self-Esteem

克里斯：世上最糟的结果，是没有自尊。为什么？

Chris: The worst outcome in the world is not having self-esteem. Why?

纳瓦尔：是啊，这是个难的。我看着那些人——我不想得罪任何人——但我看着那些不喜欢自己的人，那是最艰难的位置，因为他们总在和自己扭打，而面对外部世界已经够难了。没有人会比你喜欢自己更喜欢你。所以如果你和自己较着劲，外部世界就会变成一道跨不过去的关。人们为什么自尊心低，很难说。可能是遗传的。也可能只是境遇。很多时候我觉得是因为他们小时候没有被无条件地爱过，而那种东西会渗到很深的核心层面。但自尊问题可以是最限制人的。

Naval: Yeah, that's a tough one. Well, I look at the people—and I don't want to offend anybody—but I look at the people who don't like themselves, and that's the toughest slot, because they're always wrestling with themselves, and it's hard enough to face the outside world. And no one's going to like you more than you like yourself. So if you're struggling with yourself, then the outside world becomes an insurmountable challenge. And it's hard to say why people have low self-esteem. It might be genetic. It might just be circumstantial. A lot of times I think it's 'cause they just weren't unconditionally loved as a child, and that sort of seeps in at a deep core level. But self-esteem issues can be the most limiting.

一个有意思的想法是：在某种程度上，自尊是你在自己这里的名声。你时刻都在看着自己。你知道自己在做什么，你有自己的道德准则。每个人的道德准则不同。但如果你达不到自己的道德准则——就是你拿来衡量别人的同一套准则——它就会伤害你的自尊。所以，建立自尊的一种办法，也许就是非常严格地按自己的准则生活。先有一套，然后去达到它。

One interesting thought is that, you know, to some extent self-esteem is a reputation you have with yourself. You're watching yourself at all times. You know what you're doing, and you have your own moral code. Everyone has a different moral code. But if you don't live up to your own moral code, the same code that you hold others to, it will damage your self-esteem. So perhaps one way to build up your self-esteem is to live up to your own code very rigorously. Have one, and then live up to it.

另一种提升自尊的办法，也许是为别人做事。如果我回头看自己的人生，那些我真正感到骄傲的时刻——它们非常稀少，并不经常有，也不是你以为的那些事。不是物质成功。不是学会了这个或那个。而是当我为某个我爱的人或某件我爱的事做出牺牲的时候。讽刺的是，那才是我真正最骄傲的时候。这是通过一次明确的心理练习看出来的。但我打赌，在某个层面上，我在不自觉地把它记录下来。所以这告诉我：即便我没有被爱，创造爱的方式也是去给予爱，通过牺牲和通过责任来表达爱。所以我觉得做这样的事，能很快建立起你的自尊。

Another way to raise your self-esteem might be to do things for others. If I look back on my life, and you know what are the moments that I'm actually proud of—they're very far and few between, and it's not that often, and it's not the things you would expect. It's not the material success. It's not having learned this thing or that. It's when I made a sacrifice for somebody or something that I loved. And that's when I'm actually, ironically, most proud. Now that's through an explicit mental exercise. But I'll bet you at some level I'm recording that implicitly. So that tells me that even if I am not being loved, the way to create love is to give love, to express love through sacrifice and through duty. And so I think doing things like that can build up your self-esteem really fast.

克里斯：你谈到牺牲，这很有意思，因为很多时候人们说：「我为工作牺牲了这么多。」就像：是啊，但那是你拿自己更不想要的东西，去换更想要的东西，而不是真正承担某种代价。我在想，自尊是不是就是你守住内在的东西——你的行动和你的价值观对齐——即使困难的时候也如此，也许困难的时候更是如此。我在想，那些更内省、高正直的人是否要付出一种代价，因为你会想：你背上了这一大套间接成本，总得用某种方式付出去。

Chris: It's interesting when you talk about sacrifice, because a lot of the time people say, “I sacrificed so much for my job.” It's like, yeah, but that was you sacrificing something that you wanted less for something that you wanted more, as opposed to genuinely taking some sort of cost. And yeah, I wonder whether if self-esteem is you adhering to your internal—your actions and your values aligning—even when it's difficult, or perhaps even more so when it's difficult. I wonder whether there is a price that people who are more introspective, high integrity, pay, because you think, well, you've got this heavy set of overheads that you need to pay in some way.

纳瓦尔：嗯，如果有道德是能赚钱的，每个人都会去做，对吧？所以在某个层面上，它确实包含牺牲。但那种牺牲也可以被理解成：你在为长期而不是短期着想。比如说，美德是一套信念：如果社会里每个人作为个体都遵循它们，就会给所有人带来双赢结果。所以如果我诚实、你也诚实，我们做生意就更容易。我们互动也更容易，因为我们可以互相信任。所以即便系统里可能有几个说谎的人，只要说谎的、作弊的不太多，一个人人都诚实的高信任社会就会更好。我觉得很多美德都是这样运作的，对吧？如果我不出去和你妻子睡觉，你也不和我的睡觉，如果我不先把桌上的食物都拿走，诸如此类，那我们就能处得更好，就能玩双赢的游戏。

Naval: Well, if being ethical were profitable, everybody would do it, right? So at some level it does involve a sacrifice. But that sacrifice can also be thought of as you're thinking for the long term rather than the short term. For example, the virtues are a set of beliefs that, if everybody in society followed them as individuals, it would lead to win-win outcomes for everybody. So if I am honest and you are honest, then we can do business more easily. We can interact more easily, because we can trust each other. So even though there might be a few liars in the system, as long as there aren't too many liars and too many cheaters, a high-trust society where everybody's honest is better off. And I think a lot of the virtues work this way, right? If I don't go around sleeping with your wife and you don't sleep with mine, and if I don't take all the food that's at the table first, and so on, then we all get along better and we can play win-win games.

在博弈论里，最有名的游戏是囚徒困境。但那全是关于人人作弊，而纳什均衡——那里的稳定均衡——是人人作弊。你能玩双赢游戏的唯一办法，是有长期的、重复的回合。但那其实不是社会里最常见的游戏。最常见的游戏叫猎鹿：如果我们合作，我们可以猎下一头大鹿，两人都有丰盛的晚餐；如果我们不合作，就只好去打兔子，每人只有一小顿。所以大多数——而且那个游戏有两个稳定均衡。一个是我们都去打兔子，一个是我们去猎鹿。所以高信任社会是一个更有美德的社会：我可以信任你会来和我一起猎鹿，准时出现，把活干好，并且合理地分配。所以你想生活在这样的系统里：每个人都有自己的一套美德并遵循它们，然后我们都赢。

In game theory, the most famous game is the prisoner's dilemma. But that's all about everybody cheating, and the Nash equilibrium—the stable equilibrium there—is everybody cheats. And the only way you can play a win-win game is if you have long-term iterated moves. But that's not actually the most common game played in society. The most common game played is one called a stag hunt, where if we cooperate, we can bring down a big stag and both have big dinners, but if we don't cooperate, then we have to go hunt like rabbits, and we each have small dinners. So most of—and that game has two stable equilibriums. And one could be where we're both hunting the rabbit, and one could be where we're hunting the stag. So the high-trust society is a more virtuous society, where I can trust you to come hunt the stag with me and show up on time and do the work and divide it up properly. So you want to live in a system where everybody has their own set of virtues and follows them, and then we all win.

但我愿意说，你不必为了牺牲才这么做。你不必为了别人才这么做。你可以纯粹为自己这么做。你会有更高的自尊。你会吸引其他高美德的人。

But I would argue you don't need to do that for sacrifice. You don't need to do that for other people. You can do it just purely for yourself. You will have higher self-esteem. You will attract other high-virtue people.

克里斯：我会不会和我自己一起去猎鹿？

Chris: Would I go on a stag hunt with me?

纳瓦尔：对。是的，没错。而且如果你是那种长期释放出道德和美德信号的人，你就会吸引其他有道德、有美德的人。反之，如果你是一条鲨鱼，你最终会发现自己完全游在鲨鱼群里。那是一种不愉快的生存。而这又回到棉花糖测验的等价物。顺便说，棉花糖测验并不能复现。我最近看到它被复现危机冲击得很厉害。但它讲的是用短期去换长期。所以我觉得，对很多所谓的美德来说，做一个有美德的人，是有长期的自私理由的。

Naval: Correct. Yeah, that's right. And if you're the kind of person who long-term signals ethics and virtues, then you will attract other people who are ethical and virtuous. Whereas if you are a shark, you will eventually find yourself swimming entirely amongst sharks. And that's an unpleasant existence. But again, this goes back to the equivalent of the marshmallow test. And by the way, the marshmallow test does not replicate. I saw the replication crisis hit it hard recently. But it is about trading off the short term for the long term. And so I think for a lot of these so-called virtues, there are long-term selfish reasons to be virtuous.

克里斯：你过去和自我怀疑打过交道吗？那是不是你需要跨过的一道障碍？

Chris: Did you deal with self-doubt in the past? Is that something that was a hurdle for you to overcome?

纳瓦尔：是，也不是。我觉得我和自我怀疑打过交道，意思是：哦，我不知道自己在做什么，我需要把它弄清楚。但我没有用那种方式怀疑自己：别人比我更懂什么对我好，或者我是白痴，或者我没有价值，之类的。我想我有这个好处——我在很多爱里长大。我周围的人无条件地爱我。所以那给了我很多信心。不是那种会说「我有答案」的信心，而是那种「我会搞明白」的信心，以及我知道自己想要什么，或者说，只有我才是我想要什么的好裁决者。

Naval: Yes and no. I think I dealt with self-doubt in the sense that, oh, I don't know what I'm doing and I need to figure it out. But I didn't doubt myself in the way of: somebody else knows better than me for me, or that, you know, I'm an idiot, or I'm not worthwhile, or anything. I guess I had the benefit of—I grew up with a lot of love. The people around me loved me unconditionally. And so that just gave me a lot of confidence. Not the kind of confidence that would say I have the answer, but the kind of confidence that I will figure it out, and I know what I want, or only I am a good arbiter of what I want.

克里斯：对。那种程度的自信，我想，能让你判断：对我来说什么才要紧，我的自尊——我该不该去追这个东西？我可以对这个做出公正判断，而不是被那么轻易地摇摆。不过有一点说得特别好：即便你觉得自己没有有意识地记录自己在做的事，后台也有一些东西在记。那是不是达伊蒙？古希腊人还是谁曾经这么说的？

Chris: Yeah. That level of self-belief, I suppose, allows you to determine what is it that matters to me, my self-esteem—should I chase this thing or not? I can make a fair judgment on that, as opposed to being so swayed. But it's such a good point about even if you think you're not consciously logging the stuff that you're doing, there is some that's in the back of your mind. Was it the daimon? Is that what the ancient Greeks or something used to talk about?

纳瓦尔：对。在计算机科学里也有 daemon 这个概念，是一种一直在后台运行的程序。你看不见它。

Naval: Yeah. Also in computer science, there's a concept of a daemon, which is a program that's always running in the background. You can't see it.

克里斯：好。

Chris: Okay.

纳瓦尔：不过对，它大概来自古希腊的达伊蒙。但是——你知道、而你甚至不知道自己知道的东西，远大于你知道自己知道的东西，对吧？你知道的大多数事情，你甚至说不出来。你有一些感觉，找不到对应的词。你有一些想法，是在身体里或在潜意识里被感觉到的，你从未对自己说出来。你说不清语法规则，可你说话时毫不费力地运用它们。所以我愿意说：你的隐性知识，以及你自己也不知道的知识，远大于你能说出、能传达的知识。所以在某个层面上，你始终在看着自己。那就是你的意识，对吧？它是那个看着一切的东西，包括你的心智，包括你的身体。所以如果你想有高自尊，那就去挣得自己对自己的尊敬。

Naval: But yeah, it probably comes from the ancient Greek daimon. But yeah—what you know that you don't even know you know is far greater than what you know you know, right? You can't even articulate most of the things you know. There are feelings you have that have no words for them. There are thoughts you have that are felt within the body or subconsciously that you never articulate to yourself. You can't articulate the rules of grammar, yet you exercise them effortlessly when you speak. So I would argue that your implicit knowledge, and your knowledge that is unknown to yourself, is far greater than the knowledge you can articulate and that you can communicate. And so at some level you're always watching yourself. That's what your consciousness is, right? It's the thing that's watching everything, including your mind, including your body. So if you want to have high self-esteem, then earn your own self-respect.

克里斯：我有过这个想法：内在金规则。金规则说，你希望别人怎样对待你，你就怎样对待别人。内在金规则说，你要用别人本该怎样对待你的方式来对待自己。这或许是对那些没有在无条件的爱里长大的人的一种回敬。

Chris: I had this idea: the internal golden rule. So the golden rule says treat others the way that you want to be treated. The internal golden rule says treat yourself like others should have treated you. And it was a riposte to maybe people that didn't grow up with unconditional love.

纳瓦尔：对。从那个意义上说。关于爱，有一件有意思的事：你可以试着记起被爱的感觉。所以回到有人爱上你、或有人确实爱你的时候，真正记住那种感觉——真正和它坐在一起，试着在自己体内重新造出来。然后再到你爱某个人的感觉，到你处于爱中的时候。我甚至不一定是在说浪漫的爱。所以那里要小心一点。我说的更多是对——如果谈过去的浪漫爱情，有时会变得复杂，对吧？——兄弟姐妹或孩子之类，或父母。想一想你何时对某个人或某件事感到爱。现在：哪一种更好？我愿意说，处于爱中的感觉，其实比被爱的感觉更令人振奋。被爱有一点黏人。有一点太甜。你会有点想把那个人推开。有一点尴尬。而且你知道，如果那个人太投入，你会觉得受拘束。另一方面，处于爱中的感觉非常开阔。非常开放。它实际上让你成为更好版本的自己。它让你想成为一个更好的人。所以你随时可以创造爱。只不过是那种想要接收它的渴求。

Naval: Yeah. In that way. On the love thing, one of the interesting things about love is you can try to remember the feeling of being loved. So go back to when someone was in love with you, or someone did love you, and really remember that feeling—like really sit with it and try to recreate it within yourself. And then go to the feeling of you loving someone, and when you were in love. And I'm not even talking about romantic love necessarily. So be a little careful there. I'm talking more about like love for—it can sometimes get complex if you're talking about past romantic love, right?—a sibling or a child or something like that, or a parent. And think about when you felt love towards someone or something. And now: which is better? And I would argue that the feeling of being in love is actually more exhilarating than the feeling of being loved. Being loved is a little clawing. It's a little too sweet. You kind of want to push the person away. It's a little embarrassing. And you know that if that person is too much into it, that you feel constrained. On the other hand, the feeling of being in love is very expansive. It's very open. It actually makes you a better version of yourself. It makes you want to be a better person. And so you can create love anytime you want. It's just that craving to receive it.

为什么骄傲是最昂贵的特质

Why Pride Is The Most Expensive Trait

克里斯：那就是问题所在。最昂贵的特质是骄傲。为什么？

Chris: That's the problem. The most expensive trait is pride. How come?

纳瓦尔：哦，那是最近的一句。我发那条推，只是因为我觉得骄傲是学习的敌人。所以当我看着朋友和同事，那些仍然卡在过去、长进最少的人，是那些最骄傲的人，因为他们隐隐觉得自己已经有答案了。于是他们不想公开纠正自己。而这又回到关于名声的谈话。你被自己说过的某句话锁住。它让你出了名。你因此被人认识。现在你想转向或改变。于是骄傲阻止你说「我错了」。

Naval: Oh, that was a recent one. I tweeted that just because I think that pride is the enemy of learning. So when I look at my friends and colleagues, the ones who are still stuck in the past and have grown the least are the ones who were the proudest, because they sort of feel like they already had the answers. And so they don't want to correct themselves publicly. And so this goes back to the fame conversation. You get locked into something you said. It made you famous. You're known for that. And now you want to pivot or change. So pride prevents you from saying I'm wrong.

克里斯：在这个语境里，骄傲指的是什么？

Chris: What's pride in this context here?

纳瓦尔：它可以简单到：你在做股票交易，然后你不承认自己错了，于是死守一笔糟糕的交易。也可以是你做了一个决定，去和某人结婚，或搬到某地，或进入某个职业，结果不好，然后你不想承认自己错了，于是卡在里面。它主要是关于被困在局部最优，而不是走回去、再重新爬那座山。

Naval: It could be as simple as you're trading stocks and then you don't admit you were wrong. So you hang on to a lousy trade. It could be that you made a decision to, you know, marry someone or move somewhere or enter a profession, it doesn't work out, and then you don't want to admit that you were wrong, so you get stuck in it. It's mostly about getting trapped in local maxima, as opposed to going back down and climbing up the mountain again.

克里斯：嗯。所以它才是昂贵的特质，因为你需要继续以某种形式偿还它。

Chris: Mhm. And that's why it's an expensive trait, because you continue to need to repay it in one form or another.

纳瓦尔：对。你只是卡在一个次优点上。它会让你损失钱。它会让你损失成功，还有时间，还有时间。伟大的艺术家总有重新开始的能力，无论是保罗·西蒙、麦当娜还是 U2——我这是在暴露自己的年纪。但即便是伟大的创业者，他们也总是愿意重新开始。埃隆·马斯克的故事总让我震动：你知道，他最初是以 X.com 做后来的 PayPal。实际上，是他的金融机构被合并进了 PayPal。

Naval: Yeah. You're just stuck at a suboptimal point. It's going to cost you money. It's going to cost you success, and time, and time. The great artists always have this ability to start over, whether it's Paul Simon or Madonna or U2—and I'm dating myself a little bit. But even the great entrepreneurs, they're just always willing to start over. I'm always struck by the Elon Musk story where, you know, he did PayPal as X.com originally. Actually, it was his financial institution that got merged into PayPal.

克里斯：还好他把那个域名拿在手里。你懂我的意思吧？

Chris: It's good that you've got the domain. You know what I mean?

纳瓦尔：对，正是。我先把它停在那儿。我先拿着。

Naval: Yeah, exactly. I'll park that. I'll hold on.

克里斯：他很一致。他用这个已经用了很久。

Chris: He's consistent. He's been using it for quite a while.

纳瓦尔：他说过大致这样的话：我从出售 PayPal 里赚了两亿美元。我把一亿放进 SpaceX，八千万放进特斯拉，两千万放进 SolarCity，然后我还得借钱付房租。对吧？这人是个永远的风险承担者。他总是愿意重新开始。他对于被看成成功或被看成失败，没有任何骄傲。他愿意全部押上。

Naval: And he said something like, along the lines of: I made $200 million from the sale of PayPal. I put $100 million into SpaceX, 80 million Tesla, 20 into SolarCity, and I had to borrow money for rent. Right? This guy is a perennial risk-taker. He's always willing to start over. He doesn't have any pride about being seen as successful or being seen as a failure. He's willing to put it all in.

克里斯：每一次都再把自己押上去。

Chris: Back himself again each time.

纳瓦尔：每一次都再把自己押上去。但关键是他总是愿意重新开始，对吧？即便是现在，当他把自己的新创企业弄成了美国，对吧？他基本上是想像修自己的一家创业公司那样去修它。我觉得那是一种愿意看起来像傻瓜的意愿，也是一种愿意重新开始的意愿。很多人就是没有那个。他们成功了，或有钱了，或出名了，然后就这样了。他们卡住了。他们不想回到零。而创造任何伟大的东西都需要从零到一。那意味着你回到零，而这真的很痛苦，也很难做到。

Naval: Back himself again each time. But the key thing is he's always willing to start over, right? Even now, when he's sort of made his new startup is the USA, right? He's basically trying to fix it like he would fix one of his startups. And I think that is a willingness to look like a fool, and that is a willingness to start over. And a lot of people just don't have that. They become successful, or they become rich, or they become famous, and that's it. They're stuck. They don't want to go back to zero. And creating anything great requires zero to one. And that means you go back to zero, and that's really painful and hard to do.

辨认我们的幸福

Identifying Our Happiness

克里斯：谈到风险——有件和你有关的事，我最近想得很多。任何你过得不好、你并不真正幸福的时刻，你都没有帮到任何人。我觉得很多人已经反常地熟悉了那种默默受苦：对生活质量的期待没有设下高标准。

Chris: Talking about risk—something I've been thinking about a lot to do with you. Any moment when you're not having a good time, when you're not really happy, you're not doing anyone any favors. I think lots of people have become unusually familiar with suffering silently, in that sort of a way of not having a high bar for your expectation for quality of life.

纳瓦尔：是啊，很大一部分只是你在用梗把自己梗进一个坏结局，因为你觉得受苦不知怎么是光荣的，或者它会让你成为更好的人。或者，你知道，我以前那句打趣是：你既然这么聪明，为什么不幸福？你为什么搞不定这一件？现实是，你可以既聪明又幸福。人类历史上有不少既聪明又幸福的人。我觉得它就从这句话开始：对，你知道吗，我打算幸福。

Naval: Yeah, a lot of it is just you're memeing yourself into a bad outcome, because you think that somehow suffering is glorious, or that it makes you a better person. Or, you know, my old quip was: if you're so smart, why aren't you happy? Why can't you figure that one out? The reality is you can be smart and happy. There are plenty of people in human history who are smart and happy. And I think it just starts with saying: yeah, you know what, I'm going to be happy.

很久以前我在泰国遇见一个人，他以前为托尼·罗宾斯工作。他态度很好，我们坐在一起，他说：我有一天意识到，这世上总得有一个人是最幸福的人——那样的人必须存在。他说，为什么不是我？我来承担这个负担。我来当那个人。我听到这个，就想：哇，这相当好。这是个好框架，对吧？他知道怎么重新框定事情。所以我觉得幸福很大一部分只是一种选择，意思是你先把自己认成：其实，我要做一个将会幸福的人。我会把它搞明白。然后你一路把它搞明白。你不会丢掉其他癖性。你不会丢掉野心，或对成功的欲望。我觉得很多人有这种恐惧：哦，如果我幸福了，我就不再想成功了。不，你只会想做更契合那个幸福版本的你的事，并且你会在那些事上成功。而且相信我，幸福版本的你，不会回头看着不幸福的那个版本说：天哪，那家伙将会更成功。我希望我是他。你其实是在试图成功，好让自己幸福。那才是全部用意。你把它弄反了。

There was a guy that I met in Thailand a long time ago, and he used to work for Tony Robbins. He had a great attitude, and we were sitting around, and he said, I realized one day that someone out there had to be the happiest person in the world—like, that person just has to exist. He said, why not me? I'll take on that burden. I'll be that guy. And I heard that, I was like, wow, that's pretty good. That's a good frame, right? He knew how to reframe things. And so I think a lot of happiness is just a choice, in the sense that first you just identify yourself as: actually, I'm going to be a person that's going to be happy. I'm going to figure it out. And you just figure it out along the way. You're not going to lose your other predilections. You're not going to lose your ambition or your desire for success. I think a lot of people have this fear that, oh, if I'm happy, then I won't want to be successful. No, you'll just want to do things that are more aligned with the happy version of you, and you'll be successful at those things. And believe me, the happy version of you is not going to look back at the unhappy version and say, oh man, that guy was going to be more successful. I wish I was him. You're actually trying to be successful so you'll be happy. That's the whole point. You've gotten it backwards.

克里斯：你打开了我的一张陷阱卡。我最喜欢的洞见之一是：我们为了那个本该用来得到它的东西，牺牲了我们想要的东西本身。所以我们牺牲幸福去追求成功，好在终于足够成功的时候，才真正可以幸福。

Chris: You unlocked one of my trap cards. One of my favorite insights is that we sacrifice the thing we want for the thing that's supposed to get it. So we sacrifice happiness in order to be successful, so that when we're finally sufficiently successful, we can actually be happy.

纳瓦尔：如果你有某种联立方程，然后你把成功从两边约掉——至少在我自己的生活里，我没有发现存在这种取舍。如果说有什么的话，我发现我越幸福，就越会去做那些我擅长、也契合我的事，而那会让我更加幸福。所以我最后其实更成功，而不是更不成功。

Naval: And if you have some sort of simultaneous equation, and you just sort of stripped success off from both sides—at least in my own life, I have not found there to be a trade-off. If anything, I have found that the happier I get, the more I am going to do the things that I'm good at and aligned with, and that will make me even happier. And so I actually end up more successful, not less.

克里斯：「契合」这一点很有意思。我会尽量说得婉转一点。就我们相处过的那一点时间来说，我会说你有一种很有意思的特质：整体性的自私。你多少准备好把自己放在第一位。如果那对你是真实的，你似乎不太在乎说出或做出可能让别人有点尴尬的事。大概就是毫无歉意地优先自己。

Chris: The aligned-with thing is interesting. I'm going to try and put this across as delicately as I can. I would say, from the bit of time that we'd spent together, you have a really interesting trait of holistic selfishness. You're sort of prepared to put yourself first. You seem largely unfazed by saying or doing things that might result in other people feeling a little bit awkward, if it's truthful for you. It's like unapologetically self-prioritizing, I guess.

纳瓦尔：对，我觉得每个人都是。也许毫无歉意那一部分相对少见，但我觉得每个人都把自己放在第一位。那只是人性。你在这里是因为你活下来了。你是一个单独的有机体。

Naval: Yeah, I think everybody is. Maybe unapologetic is the part that's relatively rare, but I think everybody puts themselves first. That's just human nature. You're here because you survive. You're a separate organism.

克里斯：这有意思。我也许——

Chris: That's interesting. I'm maybe—

纳瓦尔：但我知道我们喜欢做道德表演，假装我们是为对方这么做。有多少次有人说：当然，我很乐意去参加婚礼？他们心里是：我不想出现在那场该死的婚礼上。有多少次有人说：你今天怎么样？而他们并不告诉你。

Naval: But I know we like to virtue-signal and pretend we're doing it for each other. How many times does somebody say, yeah, of course, I'd love to come to the wedding? They're like, I don't want to be at the fucking wedding. How many times does someone say, how are you doing today? And they don't tell you.

克里斯：有多少——我不去婚礼。但这就是我的要点。所以我并不认为你在这一点上必然是对的。我觉得人们确实——我不觉得他们把自己放在第一位。我有时觉得，他们会妥协自己想要的东西，好在社交上安抚眼前的人。

Chris: How many—I don't go to weddings. But this is my point. So I don't think you're necessarily right with that. I think that people do—I don't think they put themselves first. I sometimes think that they compromise what it is that they want in order to appease socially what's in front of them.

纳瓦尔：是啊。我只是把它看成：每个人都在这上面浪费时间。不要做你不想做的事。你为什么要浪费时间？这世上的时间这么少。生命过得很快。是多少来着？四千个星期。那就是你的寿命。是的，我们听过这话，但我们记不住。而我大概敏锐地意识到自己时间有多么少，所以我就是不打算浪费它。

Naval: Yeah. I just view it as everyone's wasting their time on it. Don't do something you don't want to do. Why are you wasting your time? There's so little time on this earth. Life goes fast. What is it? 4,000 weeks. That's your lifespan. And yes, we hear that, but we don't remember it. But I guess I'm keenly aware of how little time I have, so I'm just not going to waste it.

克里斯：你是怎么变得更自在地做那个毫无歉意地优先自己的人的？

Chris: How have you got more comfortable at being the unapologetic self-prioritizer?

纳瓦尔：是啊，我在这一点上变得越来越彻底、越来越不留情。主要是我看见或听见别人的自由，然后那进一步解放了我。我读过 pmarca，也就是马克·安德森的一篇博客，他说不要保有日程表，我把它记在心里。于是我删掉了日历，也不再保有日程。我试着全记在脑子里。如果我记不住，我就不会再往上加。

Naval: Yeah, I've gotten utterly more and more ruthless on it. Mainly it's that I see or hear people's freedom, and then that liberates me further. So I read a blog post by pmarca, aka Marc Andreessen, where he said don't keep a schedule, and I took that to heart. So I deleted my calendar and I don't keep a schedule. I try to remember it all in my head. If I can't remember it, I'm not going to add it.

克里斯：还好你准时到了这儿。

Chris: I'm glad you got here on time.

纳瓦尔：对，正是。我讨厌到最后一刻才去查事情。所以——不过讽刺的是，我甚至不知道马克本人是不是照这个做，但他把那一点说对了。我读过一个关于杰克·多西的小故事：他所有事务都用 iPhone 和 iPad 处理，甚至不碰 Mac，我就说，好，我也要那样。所以我要通过短信运作，不再忍受我那些讨厌的邮件。

Naval: Yeah, exactly. I hate to look things up at the last minute. So—but ironically, I don't even know if Marc himself follows that, but he made the correct point. I read a little story about Jack Dorsey doing all his business off his iPhone and iPad and not even going into a Mac, and I said, okay, I want to do that. So I'm going to operate through text messaging and not put up with my nasty email.

克里斯：那感觉更自由吗？

Chris: Does that feel like more freedom?

纳瓦尔：确实。对。因为你在路上。所以我有一个不客气的邮件自动回复，写着：我不查邮件，也别给我发短信。对。如果你需要找到我，你会找到我。显然这其中有一些是成功带来的奢侈，但这些习惯有一些我很早以前就养成了——其实那种带敌意的邮件自动回复很久以前就开始了。我以前拥有那个域名。我后来放掉了。idontdocoffee.com。我以前用那个邮箱回复，好让人明白意思。但我不再那么粗鲁了。现在我只是消失。我直接不见。我妻子知道绝不要给我预约或安排任何事。没人指望我会去夫妻晚宴。没人指望我会去生日。没人指望我会去婚礼。如果有人想拉上她、让我出席，她会说：他自己做决定。你得直接问他。

Naval: It does. Yeah. 'Cause you're on the go. So I have a nasty email autoresponder that says, I don't check email, and don't text me either. Right. If you need to find me, you'll find me. Obviously some of this is a luxury of success, but some of these habits I adopted long before—actually the hostile email autoresponder started a long time ago. I used to own the domain. I let it go. idontdocoffee.com. I used to reply from that email just so people would get the point. But I stopped being rude about it. Now I just ghost. I just disappear. My wife knows not to ever book or schedule me for anything. I'm not expected to go to couples' dinners. I'm not expected to go to birthdays. I'm not expected to go to weddings. If somebody tries to rope her into having me show up, she says he makes his own decisions. You got to ask him directly.

克里斯：反过来呢？你这不是在某种程度上杀死机缘巧合吗？

Chris: What about vice versa? Well—are you not killing serendipity in a way?

纳瓦尔：不，不。我是在腾出我全部的时间。所以我整个生活都是机缘巧合。我想和谁互动、何时互动、在哪儿互动，都可以。

Naval: No, no. I'm freeing up all my time. So my entire life is serendipity. I get to interact with whoever I want, whenever I want, wherever.

克里斯：你会听到邀请，但做出决定——因为如果进来的事更少，你就是在假定自己知道，对未来任何事什么对你最好。

Chris: You'll hear the invite, but make the decision—because if there's fewer things incoming, you're assuming that you know what's best for you into anything in the future.

纳瓦尔：所以我会说：好，如果那件事有意思，我看看那天有心情的时候能不能进去。但最糟的莫过于：过去的你替现在的你承诺了一件事，而现在的你并不想做。

Naval: So I'll say, okay, if that thing is interesting, I'll see if I can get in that day when I'm in the mood. But there's nothing worse than something coming up that your past self committed you to, that your present self doesn't want to do.

克里斯：该死的过去的我。

Chris: God damn it, past.

纳瓦尔：对。然后它毁掉你整个日历。它毁掉你的一天，因为就像：哦，这一个小时的空档像一坨屎坐在我的日历上，我得围着它安排整天。前二十分钟、后二十分钟我什么也做不成。即便是打电话，如果有人想通话，就说：好，你有空就给我发短信。我有空也给你发短信。我们现做。这比那种过度排程的 cal.com 或 iCal 之类好过得多。过度排程的生活不值得过。不值得。我觉得那是一种糟糕的活法。我们不是那样进化来的。我们不是那样长大的。我们做孩子的时候也不是那样，但愿如此——除非你碰上直升机家长或虎妈。你的自然秩序是自由。我有个朋友有一次对我说：我永远不想必须在某个特定时间出现在某个特定地点。我就想：天哪，那才是自由。我听到那句话的时候，它当场改变了我的生活。

Naval: Yeah. And then it destroys your entire calendar. It destroys your day, because there's like, oh, this one-hour slot which is sitting like a turd on my calendar that I have to like schedule my whole day around. I can't do anything the 20 minutes before, the 20 minutes afterwards. Even for phone calls, if someone wants to do a phone call, say, okay, just text me when you're free. I'll text you when I'm free. And we'll just do it on the fly. It's a much better way of living than this overly scheduled cal.com or iCal, whatever. The overscheduled life is not worth living. It's not. I think it's a terrible way to live life. That's not how we evolved. It's not how we grew up. It's not how we were as children, hopefully, unless you're a helicopter parent or a tiger mom. Your natural order is freedom. I had a friend who said to me once, you know, I never want to have to be at a specific place at a specific time. And I was like, oh my god, that's freedom. When I heard that, that changed my life right there.

克里斯：你还是不用闹钟？

Chris: You still alarm-clock-less?

纳瓦尔：对，我不用闹钟。今天我确实设了闹钟，只是为了不错过这个。

Naval: Yes, I'm alarm-clock-less. Today I did set my alarm clock just so I wouldn't miss this.

克里斯：非常重要。

Chris: Very important.

纳瓦尔：对。如果你还是——不过告诉你一声，我把闹钟设在上午 11 点，以防那天我突然感冒。我不会把闹钟设在早上 8 点或 9 点。果然，我比那个早好几个小时就起来了。但它算是一种备份应急闹钟。其实有时候有件事我需要做，我又不想看日历，我就只为它设一个闹钟。

Naval: Yeah. If you still—but just so you know, I set the alarm clock for 11:00 a.m., in case I was stricken with a flu that day. I wasn't going to set my alarm clock for 8:00 a.m. or 9:00 a.m. And sure enough, I got up many hours before that. But it was sort of a backup emergency alarm. In fact, sometimes when something that I need to do, I don't want to look at a calendar, so I'll just set an alarm for it.

克里斯：再往那种「去你的」能量里沉一点，那种优先自己的能量，因为我觉得人们在理性上很喜欢这个想法。

Chris: Just sink a little bit more into that kind of that fuck-you energy, that self-prioritizing energy, because I think people rationally love the idea of this.

纳瓦尔：我只去做我自己想做的事。即便他们已经有那种程度的自由，这也不是「去你的」能量——我的意思是，我觉得每个人都应该在最大可能的范围内那样生活。显然我们有工作和义务方面的要求，那些对我们是真正重要的。但不要把生命浪费在随机排进去的事情上，浪费在不重要、无所谓的事情上，浪费在活动和婚礼上，浪费在你不想去的、乏味的人和乏味的晚饭上。在你能把自由带进生活的范围内，为这个去优化。你其实会更有生产力。你将不只是更幸福、更自由。你会更有生产力，因为那样你就能专注于眼前的东西，无论那天最大的问题是什么。

Naval: I'm going to do what only I want to do. Even if they've got the level of freedom, it's not fuck-you energy, in the sense that I think everyone should live their life that way to the greatest extent possible. Obviously we have our requirements around work and obligations that are genuinely important to us. But don't fritter away your life on randomly scheduled things, and things that aren't important, don't matter, and events and weddings and, you know, tedious dinners with tedious people that you don't want to go to. To the extent you can bring freedom into your life, optimize for that. You'll actually be more productive. You won't just be happier and more free. You will be more productive, because then you can focus on what is in front of you, whatever the biggest problem of that day.

我早上一醒来，前四个小时是我精力最旺的时候，那是我想解决所有难题的时候。接下来四个小时，我有点想做更多户外活动，或者想锻炼，或者也许可以开一些会，但我会尽量拖到最后一刻，根据当天优先事项的要求来。最后四个小时我有点想收束。我想和孩子们待在一起，想玩游戏或读本书之类。所以拥有那种灵活和自由非常重要。那样你就能在那个时刻，把最需要的东西放进那个空档。相反，如果我下午 2 点有个会，然后我还得把一件事和一些邮件做完，我把那些拖到晚上 6 点——我就会赶。我不会有生产力。我也不会——

When I wake up in the morning, the first four hours are when I have the most energy, and that's when I want to solve all the hard problems. And the next four hours are when I kind of want to, you know, do some more outdoorsy activities, or I want to work out, or maybe I can have some meetings, but I'll try to do those last second, based on whatever the day's priorities demand. The last four hours I kind of want to wind down. I want to hang out with the kids, and I want to play games or read a book or something like that. So having that flexibility and freedom is really important. So you can just put whatever is most needed into the slot at that moment. And instead, if I have like a meeting at 2 p.m., and then I have to like get a thing and some emails done, I put that off till 6 p.m.—I'm rushing. I'm not going to be productive. I'm not going to be—

克里斯：你当然也不自由。

Chris: You're certainly not free.

纳瓦尔：我绝对不自由。不过还有一件我真正相信的事：灵感是易腐的。立刻对它采取行动。所以当你受到启发想做某件事，就做那件事。如果我受到启发想写一篇博客，我想在那个时刻写。如果我受到启发想发一条推，我想在那个时刻发。如果我受到启发想解决一个问题，我就在那个时刻解决。如果我受到启发想读一本书，我想马上读。如果我受到启发想解决一个问题，我就当场解决。如果我想学点东西，我在好奇心出现的那一刻去做。好奇心一到，我立刻去学那个东西。我下载那本书。我上谷歌。我上 ChatGPT，无论什么。我会当场把那件事弄明白，学习就是在那时发生的。它不会因为我排了时间、因为我腾出一小时就发生，因为那个时间到来时，我可能是另一种心情。我可能只想做点别的。所以我觉得自发性非常重要。你玩得开心、真正享受过程的时候，学得最好，而不是被迫坐在那儿做的时候。你还记得学校里多少东西？你知道，你被迫按这个进度、在这个时间、照这个人的安排学地理、历史、数学。没发生。所有真正留下的东西，都是你想学的时候学的，是你真正有欲望的时候学的。那种自由，那种想做的那一刻就能行动的能力，如此解放人，以至于我们大多数人过完一生，只尝到非常、非常少的一点。如果你整个生命都那样过，那就是幸福的配方。

Naval: I'm definitely not free. But also another thing that I really believe is that inspiration is perishable. Act on it immediately. So when you're inspired to do something, do that thing. If I'm inspired to write a blog post, I want to do it at that moment. If I'm inspired to send a tweet, I want to do it at that moment. If I'm inspired to solve a problem, I do it that moment. If I'm inspired to read a book, I want to read it right then. If I'm inspired to solve a problem, I solve it right there. If I want to learn something, I do it at the moment of curiosity. The moment the curiosity arrives, I go learn that thing immediately. I download the book. I get on Google. I get on ChatGPT, whatever. I will figure that thing out on the spot, and that's when the learning happens. It doesn't happen because I've scheduled time, because I've set an hour aside, because when that time arrives I might be in a different mood. I might just want to do something different. So I think that spontaneity is really important. You're going to learn best when you're having fun, when you genuinely are enjoying the process, not when you're forced to sit there and do it. How much do you remember from school? You know, you were forced to learn geography, history, mathematics on this schedule at this time according to this person. Didn't happen. All the stuff that sticks with you is you learned it when you wanted to, when you genuinely had the desire. And that freedom, that ability to act on something the moment you want to, is so liberating that most of us go through our lives with very, very little tastes of that. If you live your entire life that way, that is a recipe for happiness.

克里斯：这感觉像效率——你同时也更有效率。你拥有灵感的那一刻，将是做那件特定任务阻力最小的时候。于是：哦，我有灵感去做那个了。我把它推迟到一个我不再那么想做的时候。而在我确实想做那件事的时候，我去做另一件需要做的事，因为它在日程上。

Chris: It feels like efficiency—that you have efficient also. You have the inspiration that is going to be the most frictionless time to ever do that particular task. So, oh, I've had the inspiration to do that. I'll put that off until a time when I no longer really want to do it quite so much. And while I do want to do that thing, I'll do something else that I needed to do because it's on the schedule.

纳瓦尔：那行不通。拖延是因为你现在不想做那件事。你想做别的事。就去做那件别的事。我拒绝这种框架：效率、生产力和成功与幸福和自由相反。它们其实是一起走的。

Naval: It does not work. Procrastination is because you don't want to do that thing right now. You want to do something else. Go do that something else. I reject this frame that efficiency and productivity and success are counter to happiness and freedom. They actually go together.

克里斯：怎么一起走？

Chris: How so?

纳瓦尔：你越幸福，就越能持续做一件事，就越可能去做反过来让你更幸福的事，然后你会继续做，并且比其他所有人更能投入。你越自由，就越能更好地分配时间，就越少被义务和承诺的网缠住，就越能专注于手头的任务。

Naval: The happier you are, the more you can sustain doing something, the more likely you're going to do something that will in turn make you even happier, and you'll continue to do it and you'll outwork everybody else. The more free you are, the better you can allocate your time, and the less you're caught up in a web of obligations and commitments, and the more you can focus on the task at hand.
